import { Callout } from 'nextra/components'

# Z80N: The Next's Z80 CPU

The heart of the ZX Spectrum Next is the **Z80N**: a modern FPGA recreation of the legendary Zilog Z80, with a few carefully chosen new tricks tucked into its coat pocket. Everything that made the original Z80 great is still there: the same programming model, the same timing, the same opcodes. But the Z80N also adds the sort of conveniences that programmers in 1976 would have stared at for a while, then immediately found a use for.

This chapter looks at the Z80 and Z80N from a programmer's point of view. We will not walk through every instruction like an opcode phone book; reference manuals already do that job. Instead, we will focus on the pieces that matter when you write real Next programs: what the CPU does behind the scenes, which odd habits are visible from software, and which Z80N additions make the Next more than just a faster Spectrum.

## A Quick Look at the Registers

The Z80 gives you two complete sets of general-purpose registers, although only one set is on stage at any moment. You can swap between them in a single instruction: `EX AF,AF'` swaps the accumulator pair, and `EXX` swaps BC/DE/HL. The spare set is a backstage costume change for interrupts and tight routines: no pushing and popping a dozen bytes while the program taps its foot.

**Main registers:**

| Register | Width | Purpose |
|----------|-------|---------|
| `A` | 8-bit | Accumulator — the center of almost all arithmetic and logic |
| `F` | 8-bit | Flags — updated automatically, not directly writable |
| `BC`, `DE`, `HL` | 16-bit | General-purpose pairs, each accessible as individual 8-bit registers |

**Alternate registers:** `A'`, `F'`, `BC'`, `DE'`, `HL'` — exact mirrors of the main set, dormant until you swap.

**Special-purpose registers:**

| Register | Width | Purpose |
|----------|-------|---------|
| `PC` | 16-bit | Program counter |
| `SP` | 16-bit | Stack pointer (grows downward) |
| `IX`, `IY` | 16-bit | Index registers for displacement addressing |
| `I` | 8-bit | Interrupt vector — high byte of the IM2 handler table address |
| `R` | 8-bit | Memory refresh counter — incremented on every instruction fetch |

A few things are worth knowing before we start writing serious code. `HL` is the favorite child among the general-purpose pairs: most memory-access instructions default to it as the pointer, and it has addressing modes that `BC` and `DE` simply do not. `IX`/`IY` displacement addressing is powerful when you need it, but it costs 4-8 extra T-states per instruction compared to `HL`, so treat it like a good-but-heavy toolbox.

## How Instructions Execute

The Z80 processes instructions in **machine cycles**, each made up of T-states (clock periods). Most instructions begin with an M1 cycle, the CPU equivalent of opening the next envelope and finding out what job it has been given:

1. **M1 (opcode fetch)**: The CPU puts `PC` on the address bus, reads the opcode byte from memory, increments `PC`, and also increments `R`. The memory refresh cycle happens automatically here — one of the Z80's more elegant design choices.
2. **Subsequent cycles**: Depending on the instruction, more memory reads (for operands), write cycles, or internal calculation cycles follow.

The total cost of an instruction is measured in T-states. Simple register-to-register operations take 4 T-states. Memory reads add 3. Indexed operations with displacement can cost 19 T-states or more. That is the little tax you pay for convenience.

When writing assembly code, you can look up T-state costs quickly. Klive IDE displays timing information when you hover over an instruction in the editor, and the **Z80 Disassembly** view shows T-states for every disassembled instruction. It is a handy way to catch the innocent-looking line that is quietly eating your raster time.

> **Prefixed instructions**: The Z80 instruction space is organized using prefix bytes. `0xCB` introduces bit manipulation instructions, `0xED` introduces extended instructions (like block operations), and `0xDD`/`0xFD` switch the CPU into IX/IY addressing mode for the following instruction. The Z80N new instructions all live behind the `0xED` prefix — they co-exist with the original extended set, occupying opcode slots that the original Z80 left empty or treated as no-ops.

When the CPU encounters a `0xDD` or `0xFD` prefix, it updates an internal prefix state variable and goes back for another fetch. Prefix bytes can therefore be chained: multiple `0xDD` prefixes in a row before a valid instruction still work, even if they look like the machine code equivalent of clearing your throat.

## Interrupts: Maskable and Not

The Z80 has two interrupt pins: `/INT` (maskable) and `/NMI` (non-maskable). They are both ways to tap the CPU on the shoulder, but one is polite and one has already opened the door. This section gives you the CPU-level basics; the full Next interrupt system gets its own chapter in [Interrupts: IM1, IM2, and the Next's Multi-Source Interrupt System](/en/book/04-interrupts/).

### Maskable Interrupts (INT)

Maskable interrupts can be enabled or disabled by software using `EI` (Enable Interrupts) and `DI` (Disable Interrupts). The CPU's internal **IFF1** and **IFF2** flip-flops track whether the interrupt doorbell is currently connected.

The Z80 checks for `/INT` at the end of each completed instruction — never mid-instruction. If `/INT` is active and `IFF1` is set, the CPU accepts the interrupt:

1. Pushes the return address (`PC`) onto the stack
2. Clears `IFF1` and `IFF2` (prevents nested interrupts unless explicitly re-enabled)
3. Jumps to the handler address, which depends on the current **interrupt mode**:

| Mode | Behavior |
|------|----------|
| **IM 0** | A peripheral device places an instruction on the data bus and the CPU executes it. On ZX Spectrum, nothing does this — it behaves like IM 1. |
| **IM 1** | Always jump to `0x0038`. Simple and predictable. |
| **IM 2** | Build a 16-bit pointer from the `I` register (high byte) and a byte from the peripheral device (low byte). Read the actual handler address from that memory location. |

In practice, the ZX Spectrum runs almost exclusively in IM 1 or IM 2. IM 2 is particularly clever: you load `I` with the high byte of a jump table somewhere in RAM, and each peripheral device's interrupt points to a different handler through that table. The ZX Spectrum Next uses IM 2 so different hardware components, such as [DMA](/en/book/06-zxndma/), sprites, and frame events, can each have their own handler instead of all crowding around `0x0038`.

> **The EI backlog**: A subtle behavior that trips up plenty of low-level code — after executing `EI`, interrupts are only enabled *after* the next instruction completes. This means `EI` / `RET` (the classic interrupt handler epilogue) processes no interrupt between those two instructions.

### Non-Maskable Interrupts (NMI)

NMIs cannot be blocked by software. When `/NMI` goes active, the CPU responds at the end of the current instruction regardless of `IFF1`. This is not a request; it is the hardware walking in with a key. The sequence:

1. Saves `IFF1` into `IFF2`
2. Clears `IFF1` (maskable interrupts are now disabled)
3. Pushes `PC` onto the stack
4. Jumps unconditionally to `0x0066`

That address is hardwired: no configuration, no negotiation. To return from an NMI, the handler uses `RETN` (Return from Non-Maskable Interrupt), which restores `IFF1` from `IFF2`. A plain `RET` would move the program counter back, but it leaves the interrupt-enable state slightly wrong, which is the kind of mistake that behaves nicely until the worst possible demo.

> The ZX Spectrum Next uses NMI in interesting ways — the Multiface device triggers one to let you intercept running programs for debugging. Since NMIs are truly non-maskable, they'll fire even when the program under test has disabled interrupts. Which is exactly the point.

## The Hidden Register: WZ

Here is something Zilog never officially documented: the Z80 has an internal **16-bit register** that software cannot read directly, but can still catch leaving footprints. It is known as **MEMPTR** or **WZ**, with **W** as the high byte and **Z** as the low byte.

WZ acts as a temporary address latch. The CPU uses it for multi-step address calculations, especially when building 16-bit addresses from two consecutive byte fetches, or when certain instructions need a "next expected address" for their side effects. Think of it as the scrap of paper the CPU writes an address on while it is doing something else.

Understanding WZ matters because it affects the behavior of the `BIT` instruction in a detectable way. After `BIT n,(HL)`, the undocumented `R5` and `R3` flags (more on those in a moment) are set from bits 5 and 3 of the address held in WZ, not from the memory data that was actually read. Whether that is a design quirk or a happy accident from the silicon implementation, it is real behavior, and some old code knows how to lean on it.

Some common operations that update WZ:

- `LD A,(nn)` — `WZ = nn + 1`
- `IN A,(n)` — `WZ = (A << 8 | n) + 1`
- `OUT (n),A` — `W = A, Z = n + 1`
- `JP nn` / `CALL nn` — `WZ = nn` (the destination address)
- `JR e` — `WZ =` the computed destination
- IM 2 interrupt acceptance — `WZ` is used to assemble the handler address before it moves to `PC`

WZ is not something you explicitly load or read in assembly code. It shows up indirectly as subtle flag behavior in programs that test bits through memory, or in code that deliberately exploits MEMPTR-observable behavior. Some original Spectrum protection schemes were built around this sort of thing, because apparently being clever with undocumented behavior was part of the sport.

## Undocumented Flags

The `F` register has 8 bits, but the official Zilog documentation marks two of them as "undefined" or "unused." In practice, the silicon is perfectly consistent about what it puts there. The bits were not useless; they were just left off the guest list.

```
Bit:  7  6  5  4  3  2  1  0
Flag: S  Z  Y  H  X  PV N  C
```

Bits 5 (`Y`) and 3 (`X`) are the undocumented flags — known as `R5` and `R3`. After most arithmetic and logical operations, these bits are **copied from the corresponding bits of the result**:

- After `ADD A, n` or most arithmetic: R5 = bit 5 of result, R3 = bit 3 of result
- After `CP n`: R5 and R3 = bits 5 and 3 of the *operand* (not the subtraction result)
- After `BIT n, r`: R5 = bit 5 of operand, R3 = bit 3 of operand
- After `BIT n, (HL)`: R5 and R3 = bits 5 and 3 of **WZ.high** (the W register) — not from the memory data

That last case, `BIT n,(HL)` reading flags from WZ rather than from the memory byte, is the kind of behavior that explains why some old Z80 code seems to care about details the official manual never mentioned. For everyday programming you mostly need to know that these flags exist; for compatibility work, they become part of the real machine's personality.

## Undocumented Instructions

The Z80 has instructions that Zilog never documented, but the silicon executes reliably. They have been reverse-engineered from real hardware, and existing software uses them. Any serious Z80 implementation should support them, because "undocumented" does not mean "unused"; it often means "someone found it and shipped a game with it."

**SLL (Shift Left Logical)** — also called `SL1` by some assemblers:

```z80klive
SLL A    ; CB 37 — shift A left, insert 1 into bit 0
SLL B    ; CB 30 — same for B
```

Unlike `SLA` (which shifts left and inserts 0 in bit 0), `SLL` inserts a 1. The name implies "logical shift" but the real meaning is "shift left, set". It lives in the `CB`-prefix opcode space that Zilog left reserved, and the chip executes it anyway. Reserved space, it turns out, is not always empty space.

**Half-register access on IX and IY:**

The high and low bytes of `IX` (called `IXH` and `IXL`) and `IY` (`IYH`, `IYL`) can be used in many standard 8-bit instructions when prefixed with `DD` or `FD`:

```z80klive
LD A,IXH      ; DD 7C — load A with the high byte of IX
LD IYL,42     ; FD 2E 2A — load the low byte of IY with 42
ADD A,IXL     ; DD 85 — add the low byte of IX to A
INC IYH       ; FD 24 — increment the high byte of IY
```

This works because the `DD`/`FD` prefix replaces what the CPU treats as "H" and "L" with `IXH`/`IXL` or `IYH`/`IYL` throughout many instructions. You cannot mix half IX/IY registers with `(HL)` memory access in the same instruction; the prefix is already doing double duty and refuses a second job.

**IN (C)** — opcode `ED 70`:

```z80klive
IN (C)     ; Read from port BC, update flags, discard the data value
```

This is the undocumented variant of `IN r,(C)` where the data read from the port isn't stored, but the flags update normally based on the value. Useful for testing I/O ports for their effect on flags without loading a register.

## I/O Operations: The Full 16-Bit Address

Here is something that often surprises Z80 newcomers: when you execute `OUT (n),A` or `IN A,(n)`, the instruction appears to use an 8-bit port number, but the Z80 puts a **full 16-bit address** on the address bus. The upper byte has not gone missing; it is hiding in plain sight.

For the simple forms with an 8-bit port operand:

```
Port address = (A << 8) | n
```

The accumulator's current value forms the high byte. So `OUT (0xFE),A` when `A` holds `0x01` outputs to port address `0x01FE`, not just port `0xFE`. The WZ register is also updated: for `OUT (n),A`, WZ.high = A, WZ.low = n + 1.

For the register forms — `IN r,(C)` and `OUT (C),r` — the full `BC` register pair is the port address:

```z80klive
LD BC,0x7FFE   ; Full 16-bit port address
IN A,(C)        ; Read from port 0x7FFE
```

The block I/O instructions (`INI`, `OUTI`, `INIR`, `OTIR`, etc.) also use `BC` as the full port address.

Why does this matter? Because the ZX Spectrum's ULA is decoded on port `0xFE`, but the ULA only checks the lower address bit, not all 8 or 16. Most original Spectrum hardware decodes only the lower bits of the address bus. On the ZX Spectrum Next, hardware devices sometimes decode more bits, so your code needs to use the full port address intentionally. Get it wrong and some ports will sit there like you knocked on the neighbor's door. The next chapter, [Talking to the Hardware: I/O Ports and NextRegs](/en/book/02-io-and-nextregs/), builds directly on this.

## Block Instructions

The Z80 has four families of block operations, each with an increment variant (I) and a decrement variant (D), plus repeating forms (R) that loop until a condition is met.

**Memory-to-memory block copy:**

```z80klive
LDI     ; (DE) ← (HL), DE++, HL++, BC--
LDD     ; (DE) ← (HL), DE--, HL--, BC--
LDIR    ; Repeat LDI until BC = 0
LDDR    ; Repeat LDD until BC = 0
```

**Memory search:**

```z80klive
CPI     ; Compare A with (HL), HL++, BC--
CPD     ; Compare A with (HL), HL--, BC--
CPIR    ; Repeat CPI until BC = 0 or A matches
CPDR    ; Repeat CPD until BC = 0 or A matches
```

**Block I/O from port to memory:**

```z80klive
INI     ; (HL) ← IN(BC), B--, HL++
IND     ; (HL) ← IN(BC), B--, HL--
INIR    ; Repeat INI until B = 0
INDR    ; Repeat IND until B = 0
```

**Block I/O from memory to port:**

```z80klive
OUTI    ; OUT(BC) ← (HL), B--, HL++
OUTD    ; OUT(BC) ← (HL), B--, HL--
OTIR    ; Repeat OUTI until B = 0
OTDR    ; Repeat OUTD until B = 0
```

The repeating forms work by re-executing from the same PC position while the loop condition is still true. No separate branch instruction is needed; the loop is built into the instruction. When `BC` reaches zero, it stops looping. This is why `LDIR` achieves high throughput: it costs 21 T-states per iteration while looping, and 16 T-states on the final iteration. Both timings matter for timing-sensitive code.

> The block I/O instructions use `B` (not `BC`) as their counter, while `LDI`/`LDD` use the full `BC`. This asymmetry catches people occasionally. Also, `OUTI`/`OUTD` and their repeating forms set flags in an unusual way: the N flag reflects bit 7 of the transferred byte, and parity is calculated from the sum of the byte and `L`.

## Arithmetic: Simple by Design

The Z80's arithmetic capabilities are deliberately limited. Keeping the instruction set compact made sense in 1976: chip area was expensive, and simplicity meant lower cost and higher reliability. The result is charmingly practical, but nobody would accuse it of being a pocket calculator.

**What the Z80 can do:**
- 8-bit and 16-bit add and subtract (with or without carry for multi-byte chains)
- Increment and decrement (8-bit and 16-bit)
- Compare (subtract without storing the result — just updates flags)
- Bit rotations and shifts: `SLA`, `SRA`, `SRL`, `RLC`, `RRC`, `RL`, `RR`, and the undocumented `SLL`
- BCD adjustment with `DAA` after BCD arithmetic

**What the Z80 cannot do:**
- Multiply (not 8×8, not anything)
- Divide
- Floating-point arithmetic of any kind

Multiplication in original Z80 assembly is a loop:

```z80klive
; Multiply D by E, unsigned, result in HL
    LD HL, 0
    LD B, 8
mul_loop:
    ADD HL, HL     ; Shift result left
    SLA E          ; Shift multiplier left, high bit into carry
    JR NC, no_add
    ADD HL, DE     ; Add multiplicand
no_add:
    DJNZ mul_loop  ; Eight iterations
```

Eight iterations, roughly 13 cycles each plus overhead: somewhere around 115 T-states for an 8×8 multiply. At 3.5 MHz, that is about 33 microseconds, which is a noticeable chunk of time in a tight graphics loop. Multiply often enough and the frame starts to feel it in its knees.

The Z80N fixes this particular 1970s inconvenience with `MUL D,E`. More on that shortly.

---

## Z80N: What the Next Adds

The Z80N extensions live in previously unused `ED`-prefix opcode slots. The original Z80 treats most of these as no-ops, so Z80N code running on original hardware usually does nothing instead of crashing. That is not compatibility in the grand romantic sense, but it is a useful kind of harmless confusion.

### Nibble Manipulation

**`SWAPNIB`** / `SWAP` (opcode: `ED 23`)

Swaps the high and low nibbles of `A`:

```z80klive
LD A,0x3F     ; A = 0b0011_1111
SWAPNIB        ; A = 0b1111_0011  (= 0xF3)
```

One instruction instead of four rotations. Useful for BCD operations, palette color swaps, or reordering any packed pair of 4-bit values. Tiny instruction, tidy little magic trick.

<Callout type="info" emoji="💡">
Try the `Z80NDemo.DoSwapnib` example.
</Callout>

**`MIRROR A`** / `MIRR` (opcode: `ED 24`)

Reverses the bit order of `A` — bit 7 becomes bit 0, bit 0 becomes bit 7:

```z80klive
LD A, 0b10110001   ; 0xB1
MIRROR A           ; A = 0b10001101  (= 0x8D)
```

The classic use case is horizontal sprite flipping in 1-bit-per-pixel graphics. Without `MIRROR`, you need eight shift-and-OR operations per byte. With it, one instruction. Your sprite turns around without making the CPU do calisthenics.

<Callout type="info" emoji="💡">
Try the `Z80NDemo.DoMirror` example.
</Callout>

**`TEST n`** / `TEST` (opcode: `ED 27`)

Performs a bitwise AND between `A` and an immediate byte, updates flags, but **does not modify `A`**:

```z80klive
TEST 0x80    ; Check bit 7 of A — A is unchanged
JP NZ, bit_set
```

Think of it as `AND n` minus the side effect. It asks "would this bit pattern match?" and then leaves `A` exactly where it found it. The flag behavior is identical to `AND`: `H` is always set, `N` and `C` are cleared, `S`, `Z`, and `PV` reflect the result.

<Callout type="info" emoji="💡">
Try the `Z80NDemo.DoTest` example.
</Callout>

### Barrel Shifts

The original Z80's shift instructions move one bit at a time. Shifting by 5 positions costs five instructions, which is very honest and also a bit tedious. The Z80N adds five barrel shift operations that operate on the full 16-bit `DE` register pair, using `B` as the shift count:

**`BSLA DE,B`** / `BSLA` (opcode: `ED 28`) — Barrel Shift Left Arithmetic:

```z80klive
LD DE,0x0012
LD B,4
BSLA DE,B     ; DE = 0x0120 (shifted left 4 bits)
```

<Callout type="info" emoji="💡">
Try the `Z80NDemo.DoBsla` example.
</Callout>

**`BSRA DE,B`** / `BSRA` (opcode: `ED 29`) — Barrel Shift Right Arithmetic (sign-preserving):

```z80klive
LD DE,0xFFE0  ; Negative value (-32 in signed 16-bit)
LD B,2
BSRA DE,B     ; DE = 0xFFF8 (sign bit preserved, fills from top)
```

<Callout type="info" emoji="💡">
Try the `Z80NDemo.DoBsra` example. This example also demonstrates the sign-preserving behavior by 
showing how the top bits fill with 1s for negative values.
</Callout>

**`BSRL DE,B`** / `BSRL` (opcode: `ED 2A`) — Barrel Shift Right Logical (zero-filled):

```z80klive
LD DE,0xFFE0
LD B,2
BSRL DE,B     ; DE = 0x3FF8 (zeros fill from the top)
```

<Callout type="info" emoji="💡">
Try the `Z80NDemo.DoBsrl` example. This example also demonstrates the zero-filling behavior by 
showing how the top bits fill with 0s for positive values.
</Callout>

**`BSRF DE,B`** / `BSRF` (opcode: `ED 2B`) — Barrel Shift Right Fill (ones-filled):

```z80klive
LD DE,0x0060
LD B,2
BSRF DE,B     ; DE = 0xC018 (ones fill from the top)
```

<Callout type="info" emoji="💡">
Try the `Z80NDemo.DoBsrf` example. This example also demonstrates the ones-filling behavior by 
showing how the top bits fill with 1s.
</Callout>

**`BRLC DE,B`** / `BRLC` (opcode: `ED 2C`) — Barrel Rotate Left Circular:

```z80klive
LD DE,0x8001
LD B,1
BRLC DE,B     ; DE = 0x0003 (rotated left, top bit wraps to bottom)
```

<Callout type="info" emoji="💡">
Try the `Z80NDemo.DoBrlc` example.
</Callout>

All five operate on `DE` as a 16-bit value. The shift count in `B` is masked to 5 bits for `BSLA`/`BSRA`/`BSRL`/`BSRF` (so 0–31 are meaningful), and to 4 bits for `BRLC` (0–15). A count of 0 leaves `DE` unchanged. None of these update flags.

> These instructions shine in fixed-point arithmetic and graphics manipulation. Shifting a 16-bit coordinate or scale factor by multiple bits used to require N separate shift instructions. Now it is one instruction, and `DE` gives you the same 16-bit range as `HL` without borrowing the register pair that half the instruction set wants to use as a pointer.

### Multiplication

**`MUL D,E`** / `MUL` (opcode: `ED 30`)

Unsigned 8×8 → 16-bit multiply:

```z80klive
LD D,12
LD E,10
MUL D,E    ; DE = 120 (0x007C)
```

`D` and `E` are the two operands; the 16-bit result replaces `DE`. The operation is **unsigned**, so both operands are treated as values in the range 0-255. Maximum result: 255 × 255 = 65025 (0xFEFF), which fits in 16 bits. For signed multiplication, you handle signs separately, but unsigned multiply covers the vast majority of practical cases: sprite dimensions, color values, screen coordinates.

Flags are not affected. And unlike the software loop shown earlier, `MUL` completes in 8 T-states. It turns multiplication from a small routine into a single instruction, which is exactly the sort of upgrade graphics code notices.

### Extended Arithmetic on Register Pairs

The original Z80 can add 16-bit register pairs together, but only with `HL` as the destination and only register-to-register. The Z80N loosens the collar in two useful directions.

**Add accumulator to a register pair** (zero-extended to 16 bits):

```z80klive
ADD HL,A    ; HL = HL + A  (ED 31)
ADD DE,A    ; DE = DE + A  (ED 32)
ADD BC,A    ; BC = BC + A  (ED 33)
```

**Add a 16-bit immediate to a register pair:**

```z80klive
ADD HL,1000  ; HL = HL + 1000  (ED 34)
ADD DE,0x20  ; DE = DE + 32    (ED 35)
ADD BC,5     ; BC = BC + 5     (ED 36)
```

None of these update flags. The original `ADD HL,rr` updates carry and half-carry; these new variants leave the flags alone. That makes them safe for pointer arithmetic inside loops where you do not want to ruin the condition your next jump is about to inspect.

### Push Immediate

**`PUSH nn`** / `PUSH` (opcode: `ED 8A`)

Pushes a 16-bit literal value directly onto the stack:

```z80klive
PUSH 0x1234    ; SP -= 2, stack now holds 0x1234
```

This saves the common two-instruction sequence:

```z80klive
; Original Z80:
LD HL,0x1234
PUSH HL

; Z80N:
PUSH 0x1234    ; One instruction
```

One point worth noting: unlike every other 16-bit immediate in the Z80 instruction set, which uses little-endian byte order (low byte first), `PUSH nn` encodes its 16-bit operand **high byte first** in the instruction stream. The assembler handles this for you, but it is a quirk worth knowing if you are hand-patching binary code. It is also exactly the sort of detail that waits until 1 a.m. to matter.

### Memory Operations: Block Copies with Exclusion

The Z80N adds a family of block-copy instructions that work like `LDI`/`LDIR`/`LDD`/`LDDR`, but with a useful little trapdoor: they skip the write if the source byte equals the value in `A`. This is the **transparent color** idiom. Load `A` with your background or transparency color, and the instruction automatically skips pixels that match it.

**`LDIX`** / `LDIX` (opcode: `ED A4`) — LDI with exclusion:

```z80klive
; A = transparent color
LDIX    ; if (HL) != A: (DE) = (HL); always: HL++, DE++, BC--
```

<Callout type="info" emoji="💡">
Try the `Z80NDemo.DoLdix` example.
</Callout>

**`LDDX`** / `LDDX` (opcode: `ED AC`) — LDD with exclusion (HL decrements):

```z80klive
LDDX    ; if (HL) != A: (DE) = (HL); always: HL--, DE++, BC--
```

<Callout type="info" emoji="💡">
Try the `Z80NDemo.DoLddx` example.
</Callout>

**`LDIRX`** / `LIRX` (opcode: `ED B4`) — Repeating LDIX:

```z80klive
LDIRX   ; Repeat LDIX until BC = 0
```

<Callout type="info" emoji="💡">
Try the `Z80NDemo.DoLdirx` example.
</Callout>

**`LDDRX`** / `LDRX` (opcode: `ED BC`) — Repeating LDDX:

```z80klive
LDDRX   ; Repeat LDDX until BC = 0
```

<Callout type="info" emoji="💡">
Try the `Z80NDemo.DoLddrx` example.
</Callout>

**`LDWS`** / `LDWS` (opcode: `ED A5`) — Load Word and Swap:

An unusual one: it copies a byte from `(HL)` to `(DE)`, then increments only the **low bytes**: `L++` and `D++`. The high bytes `H` and `E` stay fixed.

```z80klive
LDWS    ; (DE) = (HL), L++, D++
```

<Callout type="info" emoji="💡">
Try the `Z80NDemo.DoLdws` example.
</Callout>

This keeps both pointers within the same 256-byte pages. It is designed for operations where you want to step through two memory areas that share their high-address byte. That sounds oddly specific because it is oddly specific, but screen and buffer manipulation often rewards exactly this kind of odd specificity.

**`LDPIRX`** / `LPRX` (opcode: `ED B7`) — Block copy from page-aligned source with exclusion:

The most specialized of the bunch. If `LDIX` is a general-purpose copy helper, `LDPIRX` is a sprite-shaped tool built for one narrow job. The source address is built from an 8-byte aligned block:

```z80klive
source = (HL & ~0x07) | (E & 7)
```

`HL` provides the block base address (rounded down to the nearest 8-byte boundary), and the lower 3 bits of `E` provide the offset within that block. As `DE` increments with each iteration, `E` cycles through the 8-byte pattern automatically. Transparent pixels (matching `A`) are skipped as with the other exclusion instructions.

```z80klive
; HL points to an 8-byte aligned sprite pattern
; DE points to the screen destination
; BC = number of pixels to copy
; A  = transparent color
LDPIRX
```

Per iteration, the instruction reads the source byte from `(HL & ~0x07) | (E & 7)`, conditionally writes it to `(DE)` if the byte differs from `A`, then always increments `DE` and decrements `BC`. `HL` is never modified; it acts as a fixed base address for the 8-byte pattern table throughout the entire loop. The lower 3 bits of `E` cycle through `0`–`7` automatically as `DE` increments after each step.

This is designed specifically for rendering 8-pixel-wide sprites: `HL` is your sprite data (8-aligned), `DE` is your screen destination. For each of `BC` iterations, the instruction reads from successive bytes of the sprite pattern, skipping any that match the transparent color. It is sprite stamping with the transparent ink already built in.

<Callout type="info" emoji="💡">
Try the `Z80NDemo.DoLdpirx` example.
</Callout>

### Screen Navigation

These three instructions exist because the ZX Spectrum's screen memory layout is... creative. The 192 pixel rows are stored in a non-linear, interleaved order: the screen is divided into three 2KB thirds, and within each third, rows are organized in 8-pixel character bands before the pixel rows. Navigating vertically in screen memory is not a simple pointer increment. It is more like using a filing cabinet designed by someone who loved television timing more than future programmers.

**`PIXELAD`** / `PXAD` (opcode: `ED 94`) — Pixel Address:

Computes the screen memory byte address for the pixel at logical coordinates (`D`, `E`) — where `D` is the row (0–191) and `E` is the column (0–255) — and loads the result into `HL`:

```z80klive
LD D,50     ; pixel row
LD E,120    ; pixel column
PIXELAD     ; HL = address of the screen byte containing this pixel
```

The formula applied internally:
```
HL = 0x4000 | (D & 0xC0) << 5 | (D & 0x07) << 8 | (D & 0x38) << 2 | (E >> 3)
```

Without `PIXELAD`, computing a screen address from (row, column) coordinates requires 8–10 instructions. With it, two loads and one instruction. The hardware does the map-folding for you.

<Callout type="info" emoji="💡">
Try the `Z80NDemo.DoPixelad` example.
</Callout>

**`PIXELDN`** / `PXDN` (opcode: `ED 93`) — Pixel Down:

Advances `HL` to the corresponding address one pixel row lower in screen memory:

```z80klive
PIXELDN    ; HL = same column, next pixel row
```

Because of the interleaved layout, "one row down" is not `HL + 32`. That would be too civilized. The instruction handles three cases:
- Not at the last pixel row of a character cell: increment `H`
- At the last pixel row but not the last character row in this screen third: add 0x20 to `L`
- At the last row of a screen third: jump to the next third

The equivalent in original Z80 assembly is eight instructions plus a conditional branch. `PIXELDN` handles all three cases in one, which is exactly the kind of small mercy you appreciate after the third screen routine.

<Callout type="info" emoji="💡">
Try the `Z80NDemo.DoPixeldn` example.
</Callout>

**`SETAE`** / `STAE` (opcode: `ED 95`) — Set A from E:

Sets `A` to a single-bit pixel mask, where the bit position within a byte is given by the lower 3 bits of `E`:

```z80klive
LD E,0     ; column within byte = 0 (leftmost)
SETAE      ; A = 0b10000000 (0x80)

LD E,5     ; column within byte = 5
SETAE      ; A = 0b00000100 (0x04)
```

<Callout type="info" emoji="💡">
Try the `Z80NDemo.DoSetae` example.
</Callout>

`PIXELAD` gives you the byte in screen memory; `SETAE` gives you the bit mask within that byte. Together, they replace what would otherwise be a lookup table or a several-instruction calculation. Address first, mask second, pixel placed.

### I/O Extension

**`OUTINB`** / `OTIB` (opcode: `ED 90`) — Output, Increment, and update flags:

Reads a byte from `(HL)`, writes it to port `BC`, then increments `HL`. Flags are updated based on the transferred byte and the current value of `B`:

```z80klive
OUTINB    ; OUT (BC) ← (HL), HL++, update flags
```

The key difference from `OUTI`: this instruction does **not** decrement `B`. `OUTI` is designed as a counter-based loop instruction, like the block instruction family. `OUTINB` is for streaming data to a port when you are managing the count separately, so it politely keeps its hands off your counter.

### Hardware Access: NEXTREG

**`NEXTREG reg,n`** / `NREG` (opcode: `ED 91`) and **`NEXTREG reg,A`** / `NREG` (opcode: `ED 92`)

These are the Z80N instructions you will use most often in Next-specific code. They write directly to the ZX Spectrum Next's hardware configuration registers (NextReg registers) without the normal two-port I/O dance.

The traditional approach to writing a NextReg:

```z80klive
LD BC,0x243B    ; NextReg select port
LD A,0x15       ; Register number
OUT (C),A
LD BC,0x253B    ; NextReg data port
LD A,0x80       ; Value to write
OUT (C),A
```

Six instructions, 12 bytes, roughly 60 T-states. Perfectly respectable, but not exactly nimble.

The Z80N approach:

```z80klive
NEXTREG 0x15,0x80    ; 4 bytes, ~20 T-states
```

When the value comes from `A`:

```z80klive
LD A,(palette_value)
NEXTREG 0x41,A       ; Write A to NextReg 0x41 (palette value register)
```

NextReg registers control everything specific to the Next: layer priorities, palette entries, memory mapping, sprite visibility, DMA configuration, clock speed, copper timing. `NEXTREG` is the instruction you reach for in virtually all Next hardware setup code. It is the short hallway to the control room.

<Callout type="info" emoji="💡">
The next chapter, [Talking to the Hardware: I/O Ports and NextRegs](/en/book/02-io-and-nextregs/), covers NextRegs in detail.
</Callout>

### Indirect Jump

**`JP (C)`** (opcode: `ED 98`) — Jump via Port:

Reads from port `BC`, then builds a jump address from the result:

```z80klive
JP (C)    ; PC = (PC & 0xC000) | (IN(BC) << 6)
```

The top two bits of `PC` (which 16KB region you're in) are preserved. The lower 14 bits of the destination are filled from the 8-bit port value shifted left by 6, so the jump target is any 64-byte-aligned address within the current 16KB region.

This is a hardware-facing instruction for page-switched environments. It allows ROM or external hardware to redirect execution by controlling what is readable from the port: a kind of software-visible ROM paging mechanism. You ask the port where to go, and the hardware points down one corridor inside the current 16KB region.

---

## Z80N Instruction Summary

| Instruction | 4-letter | Opcode | Description |
|-------------|----------|--------|-------------|
| `SWAPNIB` | `SWAP` | `ED 23` | Swap nibbles of A |
| `MIRROR A` | `MIRR A` | `ED 24` | Reverse bit order of A |
| `TEST n` | same | `ED 27` | AND n with A, update flags, keep A |
| `BSLA DE,B` | same | `ED 28` | Barrel shift DE left by B bits |
| `BSRA DE,B` | same | `ED 29` | Barrel shift DE right arithmetic (sign-extend) |
| `BSRL DE,B` | same | `ED 2A` | Barrel shift DE right logical (zero-fill) |
| `BSRF DE,B` | same | `ED 2B` | Barrel shift DE right, fill with ones |
| `BRLC DE,B` | same | `ED 2C` | Barrel rotate DE left circular |
| `MUL D,E` | same | `ED 30` | Unsigned 8×8 multiply → DE |
| `ADD HL,A` | same | `ED 31` | HL = HL + A (zero-extended) |
| `ADD DE,A` | same | `ED 32` | DE = DE + A (zero-extended) |
| `ADD BC,A` | same | `ED 33` | BC = BC + A (zero-extended) |
| `ADD HL,nn` | same | `ED 34` | HL = HL + 16-bit immediate |
| `ADD DE,nn` | same | `ED 35` | DE = DE + 16-bit immediate |
| `ADD BC,nn` | same | `ED 36` | BC = BC + 16-bit immediate |
| `PUSH nn` | same | `ED 8A` | Push 16-bit immediate value onto stack |
| `OUTINB` | `OTIB` | `ED 90` | OUT(BC) ← (HL), HL++, update flags |
| `NEXTREG r,n` | `NREG r,n` | `ED 91` | Write immediate n to NextReg register r |
| `NEXTREG r,A` | `NREG r,A` | `ED 92` | Write A to NextReg register r |
| `PIXELDN` | `PXDN` | `ED 93` | Advance HL to next pixel row in screen memory |
| `PIXELAD` | `PXAD` | `ED 94` | HL = screen byte address for pixel at (D, E) |
| `SETAE` | `STAE` | `ED 95` | A = pixel bit mask for column position E[2:0] |
| `JP (C)` | same | `ED 98` | Jump to page-relative address via port BC |
| `LDIX` | same | `ED A4` | LDI, skip write if (HL) == A |
| `LDWS` | same | `ED A5` | (DE) ← (HL), L++, D++ (page-preserving) |
| `LDDX` | same | `ED AC` | LDD, skip write if (HL) == A |
| `LDIRX` | `LIRX` | `ED B4` | Repeat LDIX until BC = 0 |
| `LDPIRX` | `LPRX` | `ED B7` | Block copy from 8-aligned source pattern, skip if == A |
| `LDDRX` | `LDRX` | `ED BC` | Repeat LDDX until BC = 0 |

- **[Talking to the Hardware: I/O Ports and NextRegs](/en/book/02-io-and-nextregs/)** — turns the I/O address rules and `NEXTREG` instruction into practical hardware configuration.
- **[Memory Architecture: ROM, RAM, Banks, and the MMU](/en/book/03-memory/)** — explains where all those pointers, pages, and screen buffers actually land in the Next's 2 MB address space.
- **[Interrupts: IM1, IM2, and the Next's Multi-Source Interrupt System](/en/book/04-interrupts/)** — expands the short interrupt overview here into the model you will use for frame timing, line 3f:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["Here is something Zilog never officially documented: the Z80 has an internal ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"16-bit register"}]," that software cannot read directly, but can still catch leaving footprints. 40:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":"WZ acts as a temporary address latch. The CPU uses it for multi-step address calculations, especially when building 16-bit addresses from two consecutive byte fetches, or when certain instructions need a “next expected address” for their side effects. Think of it as the scrap of paper the CPU writes an address on while it is doing something else."}]
41:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["Understanding WZ matters because it affects the behavior of the ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"BIT"}]," instruction in a detectable way. After ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"BIT n,(HL)"}],", the undocumented ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"R5"}]," and ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"R3"}]," flags (more on those in a moment) are set from bits 5 and 3 of the address held in WZ, not from the memory data that was actually read. Whether that is a design quirk or a happy accident from the silicon implementation, it is real behavior, and some old code knows how to lean on it."]}]

6a:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":["The repeating forms work by re-executing from the same PC position while the loop condition is still true. No separate branch instruction is needed; the loop is built into the instruction. When ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"BC"}]," reaches zero, it stops looping. This is why ",["$","code",null,{"className":"nextra-code","dir":"ltr","children":"LDIR"}]," achieves high throughput: it costs 21 T-states per iteration while looping, and 16 T-states on the final iteration. Both timings matter for timing-sensitive code."]}]

cc:["$","p",null,{"className":"x:not-first:mt-[1.25em] x:leading-7","children":"These three instructions exist because the ZX Spectrum’s screen memory layout is… creative.
